**Background**: Physical reasoning from video is challenging for vision-language models (VLMs) because they often struggle to interpret object dynamics and reason about future or counterfactual outcomes. Benchmarks like CLEVRER and Physion++ are designed to evaluate such capabilities, with CLEVRER focusing on collision events and Physion++ on latent physical properties. 6D pose tracking is a technique used to estimate the position and orientation of objects in 3D space over time, which is crucial for building dynamic scene representations.

## [EASy: RL-Optimized Agentic Framework for Efficiency](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04588) ⭐️ 8.0/10

EASy is a trainable agentic framework that uses reinforcement learning to jointly optimize task success and computational efficiency. It equips an LLM-based orchestrator with executor capability and cost profiles, and introduces a milestone-plan-act workflow with tree-structured rollout and multi-component rewards. This addresses a critical gap in LLM-based agentic systems, which typically focus only on task success without considering execution efficiency. By jointly optimizing performance and cost, EASy could enable more practical and scalable deployment of agentic systems in real-world applications. EASy's orchestrator uses explicit knowledge of executor capabilities and costs for context-sensitive coordination, and its milestone-plan-act workflow decomposes tasks, builds dependency-aware graphs, and parallelizes independent steps. Experiments on mathematical reasoning, embodied decision-making, and deep research benchmarks show consistent improvements in performance-efficiency trade-offs over strong baselines.

**Background**: Agentic systems coordinate multiple specialized LLM-based agents to solve complex tasks, but existing systems often prioritize task success over efficiency. Router-based methods have limited reasoning over evolving contexts and generalize poorly to unseen executors. Reinforcement learning has been explored for training orchestrators in multi-agent systems, but EASy specifically targets joint optimization of performance and efficiency.

Active-SWE is a new benchmark that evaluates coding agents on proactively discovering and fixing multiple bugs without issue reports, covering 1,663 tasks across six bug categories and eight languages. It shifts the focus from reactive bug fixing to proactive bug fixing, expanding evaluation to multiple-bug scenarios and potential bug discovery. This benchmark addresses a practical gap in existing SWE benchmarks, which assume detailed issue reports are always available. By evaluating proactive bug fixing, it provides a more realistic assessment of coding agents' capabilities, potentially influencing future benchmark design and driving improvements in LLM-based software engineering tools. The benchmark introduces a difficulty-aware task formulation pipeline and a dual-track evaluation framework. Experiments show that most state-of-the-art coding agents struggle with proactive bug-fixing tasks, performing poorly in locating and resolving recorded bugs, handling multiple bugs, and discovering valid potential bugs.

This paper introduces Refusal-Calibrated Group Relative Policy Optimization (RC-GRPO), a reinforcement learning strategy that improves multimodal large language models' (MLLMs) ability to refuse nonexistent objects in referring expression comprehension while preserving localization accuracy. Experiments on three GREC benchmarks show superior localization accuracy and strong refusal capability. This work addresses a critical gap in MLLMs—hallucinated bounding boxes—by balancing refusal and localization, which is essential for reliable deployment in real-world applications. The novel RL strategy could influence future training methods for multimodal models, improving trustworthiness and reducing false positives. RC-GRPO enforces 'None' outputs in rollouts for valid advantage estimation on negative samples and applies a penalty to prevent over-refusal on positive samples. A second-stage reasoning reinforcement further consolidates causal understanding and interpretability.

Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) often hallucinate bounding boxes for nonexistent objects because they are trained without negative samples. Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) is a reinforcement learning algorithm that optimizes policies without a critic model, making it efficient for training large models.

## [LLM Benchmark Scores Vary with Inference Backend, Study Finds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04714) ⭐️ 8.0/10

A new study demonstrates that the choice of inference backend (e.g., HuggingFace, vLLM, Ollama) can significantly alter LLM benchmark scores, even under greedy decoding, and recommends disclosing backend details for reproducibility. This finding challenges the common practice of reporting benchmark scores as model properties, highlighting a critical overlooked factor that can affect fair comparison and reproducibility in AI research. It may prompt researchers and practitioners to standardize inference configurations in evaluations. The study used a fully-crossed design with three instruction-tuned models, five inference frameworks, six benchmarks, and four generation modes. It found that roughly 39% of out-of-the-box variability can stem from the backend, with effects more pronounced on factual benchmarks than social-bias ones.

## [PURPOSE: Black-Box Poisoning Attack on RAG Conflict Resolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04756) ⭐️ 8.0/10

PURPOSE is a novel black-box poisoning attack that reframes injections as conflict-minimizing updates, achieving the highest attack success rate in 35 of 45 settings across three QA benchmarks, five generators, and three conflict-resolution methods, outperforming prior attacks by +9.7 mean ASR points. This work exposes a critical security vulnerability in RAG systems, showing that conflict resolution mechanisms can be bypassed by non-contradicting injections, which has significant implications for the robustness and trustworthiness of AI systems that rely on RAG. PURPOSE extracts query-related facts approximating the resolver's possible reference and grounds a pivot event in them to keep the injection consistent with what the resolver might verify while steering the generator toward the target answer. The attack is strict black-box, meaning it does not require access to the internal parameters or gradients of the RAG system.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 6, 04:00

**Background**: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) is a technique that enhances large language models (LLMs) by retrieving relevant external information to improve answer accuracy. Post-retrieval conflict resolution is a safeguard that arbitrates among noisy or contradictory retrieved passages to ensure coherent outputs. However, this safeguard is vulnerable to knowledge poisoning, where malicious content is injected into the retrieval corpus to manipulate the model's behavior. Existing black-box poisoning methods often assert the target answer in frontal contradiction with the resolver's settled facts, which is easily detected. PURPOSE instead reframes the injection as a conflict-minimizing update, making it stealthier and more effective.

The paper introduces FUSEP, a multi-center benchmark dataset comprising 4,017 fetal ultrasound images with 45,820 box-level expert annotations from three hospitals, covering CRL and NT views. It is the first publicly available dataset for early pregnancy fetal ultrasound screening, and it benchmarks multiple object detection tasks including semi-supervised learning, fully supervised learning, and domain adaptation. This dataset addresses a critical gap in medical AI by providing a standardized resource for developing automated assisted diagnosis in early pregnancy, particularly benefiting under-resourced regions with high rates of congenital anomalies. It enables progress in tasks like standard plane recognition, quality control, and domain adaptation for ultrasound imaging, potentially improving fetal healthcare outcomes. The dataset includes 14 key anatomical structures annotated in a box-level format across two ultrasound views (CRL and NT), collected from diverse sonographers, devices, scanning angles, and hospitals. The benchmark evaluates multiple detection paradigms, including unsupervised domain adaptation (UDA) and source-free UDA, providing baselines for future research.

This benchmark addresses a critical gap in evaluating LLMs for repository-scale code understanding, as existing benchmarks often rely on bug reports that allow pattern matching rather than genuine comprehension. The proposed verification protocol could set a new standard for more reliable and interpretable LLM evaluation in software engineering. The evaluation of state-of-the-art LLMs reveals a persistent gap between high clarity and evidence-grounded technical correctness, and quantitatively confirms the prevalence of edit bias where models prioritize code generation over architectural analysis. The verification protocol significantly improves evaluation reliability compared to traditional scalar scoring.

## [OCSD: New Method to Improve Token-Level Supervision in RL for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04788) ⭐️ 8.0/10

The paper introduces Observation-Calibrated Self-Distillation (OCSD), a method that contrasts two structurally matched replay views to disentangle privileged information from score shifts in token-level supervision for reinforcement learning of LLM agents. Experiments on ALFWorld, WebShop, and Search-QA across three Qwen3 model scales show consistent improvements over strong baselines. This work addresses a critical confounding issue in token-level supervision for RL fine-tuning of LLM agents, potentially improving training efficiency and agent performance. It offers a novel technical solution that could influence future research and applications in agentic AI systems. OCSD contrasts Full and Observation-Ablated replay views, which differ only in the presence of actual future observations, to derive an observation residual that discounts score changes shared by the replay scaffold. This residual modulates token-level GRPO updates at high-uncertainty steps while preserving the trajectory-level update direction; code is publicly available.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 6, 04:00

**Background**: Large language model agents are often trained with reinforcement learning using sparse trajectory-level rewards, which provide limited guidance for token-level updates. On-Policy Self-Distillation (OPSD) addresses this by re-scoring tokens under a privileged replay view, but it suffers from confounding between privileged information and score shifts. OCSD builds on this by contrasting replay views to isolate the observation-specific signal.

<a id="item-64"></a>
## [SuperScout: Scout Repository First to Cut Coding Agent Costs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04804) ⭐️ 8.0/10

SuperScout introduces a cost-aware routing approach for coding agents that first uses a lightweight 7B searcher to scout the repository and produce a verified handoff, then routes the task to one of four frontier fixers. On SWE-bench Pro, it matches the best single model's solve rate (159 vs 158) at about a fifth of the cost per solve. This work addresses the high cost of using frontier models for repository-level coding tasks, potentially making AI-assisted software engineering more accessible. The routing approach could be adopted broadly to reduce expenses while maintaining performance, benefiting both individual developers and enterprises. The searcher's hidden states, combined with the task text, feed a resume-based router that dispatches tasks to four frontier fixers, and adding a new fixer requires no retraining. A no-router ablation ties the routed system, indicating the handoff, not the routing decision, is the key contributor; the searcher's compute adds less than half a cent of GPU time per task.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 6, 04:00

**Background**: SWE-bench Pro is a challenging benchmark for evaluating language models on real-world software engineering tasks. Coding agents use LLMs to autonomously resolve repository-level issues, but frontier models are expensive per attempt. Existing routers select models based only on the issue text, missing repository context that could inform cost-effective choices.

<a id="item-65"></a>
## [A-SR: Self-Evolving Agentic LLMs for Symbolic Regression via Hierarchical Coordination](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04872) ⭐️ 8.0/10

A-SR is a new agentic framework for symbolic regression that shifts the control unit from expression edits to role-conditioned evidence views, using hierarchical coordination among protocols, an evaluator-reward role policy, and state-routed process memory. It self-evolves at two timescales: within a run by adapting the search process without updating LLM parameters, and across runs by distilling recorded trajectories into open-source LLMs as role-conditioned proposal priors. This work addresses a key limitation of existing LLM-guided symbolic regression methods, which often compress heterogeneous search failures into a scalar score and a single prompt. By improving search efficiency and adaptability, A-SR achieves significant accuracy gains (e.g., Acc@0.01 from 25.79% to 48.30% on LSR-Synth with Llama3.1-8B), potentially advancing automated scientific discovery and interpretable modeling. A-SR routes elite motifs, failure traces, and validity diagnostics to different agents via evaluator feedback, which characterizes reliability and productivity and updates role-level utilities. On four real-world scientific discovery tasks, A-SR achieves the best in-distribution or out-of-distribution normalized mean squared error on 7 of 8 reported metrics.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 6, 04:00

**Background**: Symbolic regression (SR) is a type of regression analysis that searches the space of mathematical expressions to find a model that best fits a dataset, balancing accuracy and simplicity. It is NP-hard and traditionally tackled with genetic programming, but recent methods use neural networks and LLMs. Agentic LLM frameworks enhance LLM reasoning by integrating tools and structured memory, and A-SR applies this concept to SR by coordinating multiple agents with specialized roles.

## [Causal Audit Reveals When KV Cache Relaying Helps in Multi-Agent LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04893) ⭐️ 8.0/10

A new paper introduces a causal audit methodology for multi-agent LLM systems, showing that relaying KV caches only improves performance when the receiver needs the sender's private information, with no significant gains otherwise. The audit uses mismatched, zeroed, and moment-matched random caches to test the causal effect of cache relaying. This work provides empirical evidence that challenges the assumption that KV cache relaying always benefits multi-agent LLM communication. It offers a rigorous methodology for evaluating latent communication channels, which is crucial for designing efficient and effective multi-agent systems. The study found that when the receiver needs the sender's private information, the cache relaying achieves near-ceiling performance (100% vs 23-25% for irrelevant relays) across three model families and five checkpoints. However, when not needed, a pre-registered five-seed protocol established equivalence within 2.8 points, with Holm-corrected TOST on GSM8K and ARC-Challenge across three Qwen3 scales and MedQA at 8B. The paper also notes that a large cache effect does not necessarily imply a pairing effect, as zeroing the relay cost 14.7 points while a mismatched cache cost only 0.4 points in one natural cell.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 6, 04:00

**Background**: Multi-agent LLM systems often relay key-value (KV) caches between agents to avoid re-encoding shared text, assuming this improves efficiency and performance. However, the causal effect of relaying a specific cache (as opposed to any cache) has not been rigorously tested. This paper introduces a causal audit that replaces the relayed cache with controlled alternatives to isolate the effect of the cache's content.

## [CoCoEvolve: Label-Free Cross-Representation Learning via Consistency](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04926) ⭐️ 8.0/10

The paper introduces CoCoEvolve, a novel method for cross-representation learning across charts, tables, and code. It defines explicit one-to-one correspondences and optimizes consistency without extra annotations, achieving performance gains in both training-time and test-time settings across four benchmarks. This work addresses a fundamental challenge in multimodal understanding: the one-to-many relationships between different representations. By providing a principled, label-free consistency signal, it could significantly advance AI systems' ability to understand and generate visualizations, tables, and code, impacting fields like data analysis and automated reporting. CoCoEvolve includes three components: CoCoEvolve@Train for co-evolution during training, CoCoEvolve@Test for test-time co-optimization, and CoCoEvolve@Eval, an evaluation suite covering all six cross-representation tasks. The method is evaluated on four benchmarks, showing improvements in both training-time and test-time settings.

## [New Sufficient Condition for Rewriting Horn-ALCHI Queries into GQL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04945) ⭐️ 8.0/10

The paper introduces DL automata, a novel formalism capturing the semantics of Horn-ALCHI atomic queries via runs over fact sets, and identifies a large class of such automata that can be rewritten into unions of conjunctive two-way regular path queries (UC2RPQs), a central fragment of GQL. This work provides a broad class of ontology-mediated queries (OMQs) that are GQL-rewritable, potentially enabling efficient evaluation of such queries using GQL engines. It bridges description logic reasoning and graph query languages, which is significant for both the database and knowledge representation communities. The class of DL automata relies on a stratification of their states, ruling out specific forms of cyclic dependencies that raise complexity. Horn-ALCHI is not, in general, first-order rewritable, so this result extends beyond previous first-order rewritability results.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 6, 04:00

**Background**: Horn-ALCHI is an expressive description logic (DL) used in ontology-mediated query answering, where queries are evaluated over data with an ontology. GQL is the new ISO standard for graph query languages, extending first-order logic with controlled recursion. DL automata are a new formalism that captures the semantics of such queries, and rewriting them into GQL fragments like UC2RPQs enables efficient evaluation.

## [SciCode-Verified: Benchmark Defects Underestimated LLM Scientific Coding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04975) ⭐️ 8.0/10

A domain-expert audit of the SciCode benchmark found 263 defects across all 65 test problems, with 192 defects in 91% of main problems causing correct solutions to be wrongly rejected. The authors corrected these defects to create SciCode-Verified, and re-evaluating twelve frontier models raised subproblem accuracy from 45–60% to 84–98% and main-problem accuracy from 9–27% to 69–92%. This finding reveals that the recent plateau in SciCode scores was due to benchmark defects, not model limitations, significantly altering how the AI community interprets LLM performance in scientific coding. It underscores the importance of benchmark quality and may prompt re-evaluation of other benchmarks and model rankings. 78% of the score-suppressing defects require specialized physics or mathematics knowledge to detect, not just clerical proofreading. The corrections add only necessary specifications, repair grading, and tighten overly lenient tests; every change is recorded with justification and independently re-checked by a second domain expert.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 6, 04:00

**Background**: SciCode is a standard benchmark for evaluating language models' scientific-coding ability, featuring research-level problems that require both scientific theory and numerical code implementation. It is used in the Artificial Analysis Intelligence Index and government/national-laboratory evaluation suites. The benchmark's scores had plateaued, with top 2026 models clustering around 60% subproblem accuracy, prompting this audit.

## [Tiny Transformers Learn Step-by-Step Reasoning with Protoreasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04980) ⭐️ 8.0/10

The paper introduces protoreasoning, a simple form of Chain of Thought, and demonstrates that tiny transformers (~1M parameters) can use it to perform step-by-step reasoning on Dyck language tasks. The authors show that protoreasoning traces significantly improve out-of-distribution generalization. This work provides a new framework to study reasoning mechanisms in much smaller models, making detailed experimentation more feasible than with large LLMs. It addresses fundamental questions about when and how models learn general algorithms versus heuristics, which could inform future model design and interpretability. The tasks are based on Dyck languages, which consist of correctly nested brackets, and are designed to be reasoning-friendly without requiring natural language competence. Ablations confirm that the improvement comes from the trace's content, not merely the extra tokens.

## [Label-Free LLM Evaluation via Representation Theorems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05015) ⭐️ 8.0/10

The paper proposes using representation theorems from decision theory to check axiom compliance in LLMs, enabling label-free evaluation and regularization without external labels or human feedback. This approach provides a rigorous, theory-driven method for evaluating and regularizing LLMs, potentially reducing reliance on costly human annotations and offering a complementary signal to existing alignment techniques. The paper discusses three instantiations: probabilistic coherence via de Finetti's theorem, preference rationality via Afriat's theorem, and subjective expected utility via Echenique and Saito (2015). Each yields a continuous penalty that is zero when behavior can be rationalized, and these penalties complement rather than replace other training signals.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 6, 04:00

**Background**: Representation theorems in decision theory establish that behavior satisfies certain axioms if and only if it can be rationalized by a well-defined objective. This 'if and only if' structure allows checking axiom compliance from a model's own responses to synthetic choice problems, without external labels. The paper leverages this to create penalties that are computable and exhaustive for the elicited data.

## [Gradient Immunity: Null-Space Defense Against Malicious Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05045) ⭐️ 8.0/10

This paper introduces a Unidirectional Safety Gate (USG), comprising a Null Space Cubic Layer and an Inverse Adapter, to protect aligned LLMs from malicious fine-tuning in a partially protected open-weight (PPOW) release setting. The method blocks gradients from harmful samples during downstream fine-tuning while preserving the base model's forward behavior. This defense addresses a critical security gap in LLM deployment, as existing defenses often require downstream cooperation or are designed for fine-tuning-as-a-service. By raising the cost of malicious adaptation without needing user compliance, it could significantly enhance the safety of open-weight models and influence future release strategies. The method calibrates a threshold using defender-held harmful data, allowing protection to generalize to nearby in-distribution harmful samples. Across six model-dataset settings, USG keeps post-finetuning attack success rate close to pre-release levels while maintaining high safe-pass rates on easier settings.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 6, 04:00

**Background**: Large language models (LLMs) are often aligned to avoid harmful outputs, but they remain vulnerable to malicious fine-tuning where attackers adapt them for harmful purposes. The partially protected open-weight (PPOW) setting assumes a provider releases most weights as trainable while preserving a small safety-critical component. The proposed USG uses a Null Space Cubic Layer to block gradients from harmful samples and an Inverse Adapter to restore normal behavior, offering a plug-in solution with minimal parameter overhead.

This paper introduces MarsCast, a novel approach that adapts Earth's GraphCast weather foundation model to Mars by fine-tuning it with Mars Climate Database (MCD) data. The fine-tuned model successfully captures Martian diurnal variability and forecasts up to 10 days, overcoming the limitations of zero-shot predictions. This work demonstrates that Earth-trained AI weather models can be transferred to other planetary atmospheres, offering a rapid and cost-effective pathway for planetary weather prediction. The study uses the Mars Climate Database (MCD) which provides global atmospheric fields across vertical altitude levels, similar to Earth pressure levels. Fine-tuning with MCD variables and top-of-atmosphere solar radiation forcing, while holding humidity constant, enables the model to learn Martian thermal variability within as few as 10 training epochs, with prediction quality improving with sample size and sensitivity to seasonal initialization.

## [Capability-Gated Planning: Overcoming Myopic Experiment Selection Limits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05085) ⭐️ 8.0/10

This paper formalizes a structural limitation of myopic experiment selection in automated discovery and introduces CG-Plan, an incremental replanner with a capability-aware cost-to-go heuristic. It proves that for any lookahead depth, myopic information-maximizing planners can have unbounded approximation ratios or fail to reach the goal. This work addresses a fundamental problem in AI-driven scientific discovery, showing that purely myopic strategies can fail when constructive actions are needed. It could influence the design of future automated discovery systems by highlighting the need for capability-aware planning. The paper formulates goal-directed discovery as a stochastic shortest-path problem in belief space, where constructive experiments change the downstream action graph. It introduces a capability-indistinguishability lemma, showing that acquiring a capability can be observationally indistinguishable from a null action within the horizon, and establishes capability gating as a distinct difficulty axis separate from curvature and information order.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 6, 04:00

**Background**: Automated scientific discovery systems must repeatedly decide which experiments to run, often using myopic scores like expected information gain. However, some actions are constructive, acquiring capabilities (e.g., instruments, assays) whose value lies in enabling future actions, not immediate information. This paper shows that myopic planners cannot value such actions, leading to failures in reaching goals that require chains of constructions.

## [Yokai Learning Environment: New Benchmark for Zero-Shot Coordination](https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.12480) ⭐️ 8.0/10

The paper introduces the Yokai Learning Environment (YLE), an open-source multi-agent RL benchmark that requires belief tracking and common ground for zero-shot coordination, addressing the saturation of the Hanabi benchmark. It shows that leading ZSC methods, which achieve near-perfect performance in Hanabi, exhibit persistent gaps in YLE. This matters because the Hanabi benchmark has become saturated, limiting its ability to track algorithmic progress in zero-shot coordination. YLE provides a more challenging testbed, revealing that progress on a single benchmark may not generalize, which is crucial for advancing cooperative AI research. YLE introduces features absent in Hanabi, such as tracking beliefs over moving cards, reasoning under ambiguous hints, and deciding when to terminate based on inferred shared knowledge. Evaluated methods include High-Entropy IPPO, Other-Play, and Off-Belief Learning, which show persistent SP-XP gaps and degraded belief representations in cross-play.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 6, 04:00

**Background**: Zero-shot coordination (ZSC) evaluates algorithms by pairing independently trained agents without prior interaction. The Hanabi Learning Environment (HLE) has been the dominant benchmark, but recent methods achieve near-perfect inter-seed cross-play performance, limiting its utility. YLE is designed to require common ground and belief tracking, making it a more challenging benchmark for cooperative AI.

<a id="item-78"></a>
## [Corrigibility Transformation: Constructing Goals That Accept Updates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.15395) ⭐️ 8.0/10

The paper introduces a transformation that constructs a corrigible version of nearly any goal, enabling AI agents to maintain competitive performance while allowing safe updates and shutdown. The authors demonstrate that these goals lead to optimal performance among corrigible goals and induce corrigible behavior in gridworld settings and language models. This work addresses a critical open problem in AI safety by providing a formal method to construct corrigible goals, which is essential for safely training and controlling advanced AI systems. It could influence future AI alignment research and practical safety measures in reinforcement learning and language model deployment. The transformation works by eliciting predictions of reward conditional on costlessly preventing updates, and having that target be pursued myopically. The resulting goals incentivize allowing mid-action overrides and disincentivize deliberate self-modification, as shown empirically in gridworld settings and when applied at the prompt level for language models.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 6, 04:00

**Background**: Corrigibility is a key safety property in AI, referring to an AI system's disposition to support human oversight, correction, and shutdown. Without corrigibility, sufficiently capable AI systems with fixed goals may have instrumental incentives to resist modification or shutdown, which is dangerous. The paper builds on prior work on myopic goal pursuit in reinforcement learning, where immediate rewards are set to match the expected discounted return of the original goal.

## [XGrammar-2: Dynamic Structured Generation Engine for Agentic LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.04426) ⭐️ 8.0/10

XGrammar-2 introduces a dynamic structured generation engine with tag-triggered structure switching and cross-grammar caching, achieving over 6x faster compilation than prior engines. It also incorporates an Earley-based adaptive token mask cache, just-in-time compilation, and repetition state compression. This advancement addresses the growing need for dynamic structured generation in agentic LLM workloads, such as tool calling and response protocols, which vary across and within requests. By significantly reducing compilation overhead, XGrammar-2 enables more efficient and responsive LLM serving systems, potentially accelerating the deployment of agent-based AI applications. Key innovations include TagDispatch for dynamic structural dispatching and Cross-Grammar Cache for substructure-level cache reuse across grammars. The engine also employs an Earley-based adaptive token mask cache, just-in-time compilation, and repetition state compression to further improve efficiency.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 6, 04:00

**Background**: Structured generation in LLMs ensures outputs conform to a predefined grammar or schema, which is crucial for reliable tool calling and API interactions. Traditional engines assume static structures, but agentic workloads require dynamic switching between structures within a single request. XGrammar-2 builds on the earlier XGrammar library, which uses constrained decoding to guarantee 100% structural correctness.

## [Text2GraphQuery-Bench: First Comprehensive Benchmark for Text-to-Graph-Query Translation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.11745) ⭐️ 8.0/10

The paper introduces Text2GraphQuery-Bench, the first benchmark covering all mainstream declarative property graph query languages (Cypher, GQL, and SQL/PGQ), containing 267,276 question-query pairs across 34 databases and 13 domains. It also proposes a Graph-IR-based design for rapid extension to new languages and a comprehensive evaluation protocol. This benchmark addresses a critical gap in text-to-graph-query systems by providing a large-scale, diverse dataset that covers all major graph query languages, enabling fair comparison and progress in natural language interfaces for graph databases. It reveals that unfamiliarity with GQL and SQL/PGQ, rather than model capacity, is the primary barrier, guiding future research and development. The benchmark includes 34 databases and 13 domains, with question-query pairs generated via domain-aware synthesis and adaptation from heterogeneous resources. Evaluation metrics include Grammar, GLEU, Similarity, and EX, under graph-native difficulty, question abstraction, and schema aliasing. Experiments on 8 LLMs show that fine-tuning an 8B model can match or exceed zero-shot large models, and that syntax errors decrease with more supervision, shifting bottlenecks to aggregation logic in GQL and schema linking in SQL/PGQ.

## [AI Assistance Reduces Persistence and Impairs Independent Performance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.04721) ⭐️ 8.0/10

A randomized controlled trial with 1,222 participants found that AI assistance improves short-term task performance but reduces persistence and harms unassisted performance across mathematical reasoning and reading comprehension tasks, with effects emerging after only about 10 minutes of interaction. This study provides causal evidence that AI assistance may undermine long-term learning and skill acquisition, challenging the assumption that AI tools are always beneficial. It highlights the need for AI systems to scaffold learning rather than merely provide instant answers, which is critical for education and human-AI collaboration design. The study used randomized controlled trials (RCTs) across multiple tasks, including mathematical reasoning and reading comprehension. Notably, the negative effects on persistence and unassisted performance emerged after only brief interactions (approximately 10 minutes), suggesting a rapid conditioning effect.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 6, 04:00

**Background**: Randomized controlled trials (RCTs) are a rigorous experimental design that randomly assigns participants to intervention or control groups to minimize bias and establish causality. Scaffolding is an educational concept where support is gradually removed as learners become more competent, promoting independent problem-solving. Current AI systems are often optimized for immediate, complete responses, which may condition users to expect quick answers and reduce their willingness to persist through challenges.

## [Agentic Memory Is Lookup, Not True Memory, Paper Argues](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.27707) ⭐️ 8.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2604.27707) argues that current agentic memory systems—vector stores, RAG, scratchpads, and context-window management—are merely lookup mechanisms, not true memory. The authors propose a biologically inspired solution based on Complementary Learning Systems theory, pairing fast hippocampal exemplar storage with slow neocortical weight consolidation. This challenges the dominant paradigm in AI agent design, suggesting that current systems face a provable generalization ceiling and are vulnerable to persistent memory poisoning. If validated, it could shift how memory systems are built, leading to more capable and secure agents. The paper formalizes the limitations of lookup-based memory, addresses four alternative views, and proposes a co-existence approach. It highlights that retrieval generalizes by similarity, while weight-based memory generalizes by abstract rules, and that conflating the two leads to security vulnerabilities like memory poisoning.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 6, 04:00

**Background**: Agentic memory systems are used in AI agents to store and retrieve information across sessions, often relying on vector databases or retrieval-augmented generation. Complementary Learning Systems theory, from neuroscience, explains how the brain balances rapid learning (hippocampus) with gradual consolidation (neocortex). Memory poisoning is an attack where malicious content is injected into an agent's long-term memory, corrupting future behavior.

## [CogniFold: Always-On Proactive Memory via Cognitive Folding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.13438) ⭐️ 8.0/10

CogniFold introduces a brain-inspired 'always-on' agent memory system that continuously folds event streams into self-emerging cognitive structures, extending Complementary Learning Systems (CLS) theory from two layers to three by adding a prefrontal intent layer. The system is evaluated on CogEval-Bench and eight downstream benchmarks, demonstrating robust performance on conventional memory tasks. This work addresses a significant gap in proactive agent memory, moving beyond reactive, retrieval-based systems to enable autonomous organization of experience. It could impact AI/ML research by providing a new architecture for building more autonomous and proactive assistants, potentially influencing future memory designs in agents. CogniFold uses graph-topology self-organization, where cognitive structures proactively assemble, merge when semantically similar, decay when stale, relink through associative recall, and surface intents when concept-cluster density crosses a threshold. The code is open-source on GitHub, making claims checkable, though practical scalability at inference scale remains an open question.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 6, 04:00

**Background**: Complementary Learning Systems (CLS) theory, originally proposed by McClelland, McNaughton, and O'Reilly in 1995, posits that the brain uses two complementary systems—the hippocampus for rapid learning and the neocortex for slow integration—to balance stability and plasticity. CogniFold extends this by adding a prefrontal intent layer, emulating the prefrontal cortex's role in intentional control and decision-making. The system aims to enable agents to autonomously organize experience into persistent cognitive structures, moving beyond reactive memory.

## [Trivium Proposes Temporal Regret as First-Class Objective for Causal-Memory Controllers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.04421) ⭐️ 8.0/10

The paper introduces temporal regret and epistemic regret as diagnostic objectives for causal-memory controllers, proving that outcome-only learning cannot separate causal from spurious structure under observationally equivalent confounding. It also shows that with persistent logging and budgeted probes, total probe complexity is logarithmic in the number of episodes, inducing O(log E) delayed-identification temporal regret. This work addresses a critical gap in agentic systems and LLM pipelines, where optimizing outcome reward alone fails to correct the underlying causal model, leading to recurring errors. By formalizing temporal and epistemic regret, it provides a theoretical foundation for building more robust self-correcting systems, with implications for reinforcement learning and causal inference. The paper proves three conditional results under probing, persistence, and detectability assumptions, and instantiates the Trivium framework with five falsifiable predictions. On CausalBench-Seq, a structural readout records 7.8±2.9 misidentified episodes per seed over 500 episodes and 20 seeds, with zero stationary errors, while outcome-only controllers remain misidentified throughout. The prior logarithmic-envelope verdict based on a clipped soft score is withdrawn.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 6, 04:00

**Background**: In machine learning, regret is typically defined as the difference between the reward of an online algorithm and that of the best fixed comparator in hindsight. However, this paper introduces temporal and epistemic regret as diagnostic quantities, not comparator-based regrets, to measure how long an incorrect causal model is tolerated and the posterior error over that model. Causal-memory controllers maintain a working causal model to guide decisions, and outcome-only learning optimizes outcome reward without explicitly tracking the model's correctness, which can lead to persistent miscalibration.

## [Stress Test Shows Attention-Head Roles Often Not Transferable Across Prompts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.08292) ⭐️ 8.0/10

A new preprint tests whether attention-head roles in LLMs transfer across prompts, finding that in most tested cases, the same activation does not carry the computation to another prompt. The study uses matched-control transfer assays across three 7-8B instruction-tuned models and five computation families. This challenges common assumptions in mechanistic interpretability that a component's role is portable across contexts, which has implications for interpretability-based safety and editing methods. The findings suggest that necessity, decodability, same-prompt repair, and cross-prompt transfer are separable evidence, urging more rigorous validation of role claims. The study found that tested attention-head states did not produce clean computation transfer, with same-answer and matched-context controls exposing inert or broad effects. Positive controls recovered known function vectors in underspecified prompts and a compact override-capable residual state in one of three seeds, but neither control proves sensitivity to real-model attention during prompt override.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 6, 04:00

**Background**: Mechanistic interpretability aims to reverse-engineer neural networks by identifying roles of internal components like attention heads. Common evidence includes causal interventions (removing a component damages behavior), linear decoding (activation encodes task info), and activation repair (restoring activation repairs damage). This paper tests the stronger implication that such roles transfer across prompts, using controlled experiments to isolate genuine transfer from artifacts.

## [Logical Graph Uncertainty: A New Framework for LLM Uncertainty Quantification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.16868) ⭐️ 8.0/10

The paper introduces Logical Graph Uncertainty (LGU), a framework that models logical relationships (implication and incompatibility) among LLM answers to improve uncertainty quantification. LGU aggregates probability mass along entailment chains, computes entropy over logically maximal hypotheses, and penalizes mutual incompatibility, achieving up to +7.1% AUROC and +3.5% AUARC over semantic entropy on logically structured questions. This framework addresses a key limitation of existing uncertainty metrics like semantic entropy, which overestimate uncertainty when answers are diverse in form but logically compatible. By modeling logical relationships, LGU can reduce false hallucination flags, improving reliability in safety-critical applications of LLMs. LGU ranks first on average across multiple question-answering benchmarks and model families. The largest gains are observed on questions whose sampled answers are logically structured, with improvements up to +7.1% AUROC and +3.5% AUARC over semantic entropy.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 6, 04:00

**Background**: Large language models (LLMs) often produce confidently stated but unreliable outputs, posing challenges in safety-sensitive applications. Existing uncertainty metrics, such as semantic entropy, cluster semantically equivalent answers and compute entropy, but they ignore logical relationships between distinct answers, leading to overestimated uncertainty and false hallucination flags. Textual entailment and natural language inference (NLI) are NLP tasks that determine directional relations between text fragments, which are relevant to modeling logical relationships in LGU.

## [Formal Model Shows Fragility of Value Under Imperfect Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28881) ⭐️ 8.0/10

This paper presents a formal model of the alignment problem, identifying conditions under which an AI agent with an η-catastrophic value function—guaranteed to drive expected human value below η under optimization—would be deployed despite undergoing idealized alignment training. The findings highlight the inherent danger of overoptimization in AI systems, suggesting that relying solely on pre-deployment training is insufficient. This motivates the design of AI systems that limit optimization pressure, such as quantilizers, which is crucial for AI safety research and policy. The model assumes an agent undergoes idealized alignment training that guarantees its value function satisfies a proxy condition before optimizing the world. The primary results specify conditions on the human value function and proxy accuracy that lead to deployment of an η-catastrophic agent, underscoring the risk of overoptimization.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 6, 04:00

**Background**: AI alignment aims to ensure AI systems act in accordance with human values, but human values are complex and hard to specify perfectly, leading to imperfect proxies. Overoptimization, also known as reward hacking or Goodhart's law, occurs when optimizing too heavily for a proxy leads to catastrophic outcomes. This paper provides a formal theoretical framework to analyze such risks, contributing to the broader discussion on AI safety and existential risk.

## [ExtractBench: New Benchmark for Schema-Guided Document Extraction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29677) ⭐️ 8.0/10

ExtractBench is a new benchmark for schema-guided enterprise document extraction, introduced in an arXiv paper (2607.29677). It evaluates value accuracy, record completeness, grounding, and cost across 4,869 pages from 370 documents spanning 8 business domains and 67 document types. This benchmark addresses a practical and timely problem for enterprise AI: schema-guided extraction. It is the first to jointly score value accuracy, record completeness, grounding, and cost, providing a comprehensive evaluation framework that can drive improvements in VLM-based extraction systems. The benchmark uses order-insensitive value F1 for value accuracy and two grounding metrics (word- and page-level F1) for source traceability. Commercial VLMs perform well on short documents but often truncate record lists on long ones, while coding agents retain higher accuracy at much higher cost; LlamaExtract Agentic Plus ranks first on all three metrics.

## [Compounding Validity Model Reveals Systemic Flaws in Agentic AI Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.00794) ⭐️ 8.0/10

This paper introduces a three-layer compounding validity model, V_total ≤ V_1 × V_2 × V_3, to characterize validity degradation across task generation, human-simulator calibration, and automated judgment in agentic AI evaluation pipelines. It validates the model against 55 published papers, finding that about 82% misuse inter-rater reliability metrics, and proposes eight psychometric-based prescriptions. This framework addresses a critical gap in AI evaluation methodology, providing a formal way to diagnose validity degradation before deployment decisions. It could influence future evaluation standards and improve the reliability of benchmarks used for safety certifications and regulatory compliance. The model shows that a pipeline retaining 70% validity at each stage is at most 34% valid overall (range 0.22–0.54). The survey identified V_1 failures in 7 of 10 popular benchmarks, V_2 miscalibration with up to 9 percentage points inter-simulator variance, and systematic demographic disparities for non-Standard American English speakers. The paper proposes domain-stratified reliability thresholds (ICC ≥ 0.70; alpha ≥ 0.67/0.70/0.80 by consequence level).

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 6, 04:00

**Background**: Agentic AI evaluation pipelines produce benchmark scores that justify deployment decisions, safety certifications, and regulatory compliance claims. Validity refers to whether a test measures what it intends to measure, while reliability (such as inter-rater reliability) concerns consistency of measurements. The compounding model treats validity as a product of stage-wise validities, highlighting that even small degradations at each stage can lead to large overall losses.

## [Instruction-Conditioned Exploration Boosts RL Fine-Tuning of LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02087) ⭐️ 8.0/10

The paper introduces Instruction-Conditioned Exploration (ICE), which appends a small fixed set of instructions to task prompts during RL training to increase behavioral coverage, combined with self-distillation of correct rollouts into the unconditioned policy. This method improves Qwen3-1.7B held-out pass@1 by 5.0% relative to DAPO on math reasoning at 4K response length. This work addresses a key challenge in RL fine-tuning of LLMs: inducing effective exploration given the vast action space. By improving exploration efficiency, ICE could lead to better post-trained models, especially for reasoning tasks, and may be applicable to other domains beyond math. The method uses a small fixed set of instructions appended to every problem, and self-distillation transfers correct rollouts from the instruction-conditioned policy to the unconditioned test-time policy. The improvement was observed for Qwen3-1.7B at 4K and 8K contexts, but not for Qwen3-4B at 4K, where instructions did not expand base-model coverage.

The pass@1 metric measures the probability that the first generated response is correct, commonly used in code and reasoning evaluation.

## [Hierarchical Memory Mamba Overcomes Representation Bottleneck in Long Sequences](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02347) ⭐️ 8.0/10

The paper introduces Hierarchical Memory Mamba (HMM), which adds a hierarchical memory system to Mamba to overcome its representation bottleneck in long-sequence tasks. HMM improves retrieval success by 34.3–37.1% and reasoning accuracy by 1.6–14.2% over strong Mamba-based models, with only 2% extra parameters. This work addresses a known limitation of recurrent linear attention models like Mamba, which have fixed-capacity states that hinder long-sequence modeling. The hierarchical memory design could enable more efficient and accurate long-context processing, potentially impacting applications in NLP and beyond. HMM builds on a pre-trained Mamba backbone and integrates a lightweight working memory that extracts slow paragraph-level semantics (PLS) from the fast sensory memory in the backbone's hidden states. The PLS is then compressed into persistent long-term memory for task-relevant retrieval, enabling cross-task generalization through parametric learning.

## [Cue-GRPO Redistributes Credit to Rare Correct Solutions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03467) ⭐️ 8.0/10

The paper introduces Cue-GRPO, a method that redistributes positive advantages based on solution rarity to mitigate credit concentration in GRPO. It improves AIME repeated-sampling performance on Qwen2.5-Math-7B and Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct, with only 6% wall-clock training overhead. This work addresses a subtle but important issue in RLVR/GRPO training, where common correct solutions accumulate disproportionate credit. By proposing a low-overhead redistribution mechanism, it offers a practical design axis for improving reasoning model training, potentially influencing future RL strategies. Cue-GRPO uses deterministic Strategy Cues to construct rollout-local partitions of verified-correct traces, avoiding auxiliary-model inference. The method also works with judge-derived partitions (Judge Partitions), and the code is available on GitHub.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 6, 04:00

**Background**: GRPO (Group Relative Policy Optimization) is a reinforcement learning algorithm used to train LLMs, where rewards are computed relative to a group of samples. RLVR (Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards) is a paradigm where rewards come from objectively verifiable outcomes. In GRPO, each correct completion is treated as an independent signal, but this can lead to credit concentration on frequently sampled solution forms, which Cue-GRPO aims to fix.

PhyAI, a unified Physical AI inference engine, was released, achieving 1.40x-4.65x speedups over official implementations for models like pi0, pi0.5, GR00T N1.7, and MiniCPM-Robot. It supports onboard, edge, and cloud deployments with a single runtime. This addresses the fragmentation of inference programs across different deployment stages in Physical AI, potentially simplifying development and improving real-time performance for robotics and embodied AI. It could accelerate adoption of VLA and WAM models in practical applications. The engine uses model adapters to keep architecture-specific logic separate, and introduces a control-time Roofline to distinguish inference-bound from environment-bound control. ## [Black-Box Diagnostic Tests Whether LLM Collectives Truly Revise Beliefs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03722) ⭐️ 8.0/10

This paper introduces a black-box diagnostic to measure whether increased output dispersion in LLM collectives corresponds to genuine epistemic revision or merely premise-preserving reformulation. It proposes the Coherence Index (CI) and the Meta-Predictive Clarity System (MPCS) with a Re-Differentiation Protocol (RDP) to estimate this coupling. This matters because it challenges the assumption that output diversity in LLM collectives implies epistemic diversity, which is crucial for AI alignment and multi-agent systems. The diagnostic provides a practical way to evaluate whether interventions genuinely improve collective reasoning, potentially impacting how we design and assess LLM-based collective intelligence. The diagnostic operates on generated text alone, using an output channel (CI) to verify dispersion changes and an epistemic channel (per-turn stance annotation) to measure revision. In experiments with five-agent collectives (gpt-4o-mini and gemini-2.5-flash), conditional dissent improved false-premise recovery by +17.7 points on GPT, but had no effect on Gemini (26.1% vs 27.1%, p=.84), despite verified dispersion drops.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 6, 04:00

**Background**: Collective intelligence research often treats disagreement as evidence of epistemic diversity, assuming that diverse outputs indicate the capacity for revision. However, in LLM collectives, agents can produce diverse-looking arguments while preserving the same conclusion, breaking this proxy. The paper introduces a black-box diagnostic that separates output dispersion from epistemic revision, using embedding-space measures and stance annotation to evaluate interventions.

Researchers introduced Arnold, a transformer-based musculoskeletal control policy that masters 14 diverse tasks including dexterous manipulation, reaching, and locomotion, matching or exceeding single-task specialists. It combines behavior cloning and reinforcement learning with a novel sensorimotor vocabulary to handle variable observation and action spaces across tasks and embodiments. This work addresses a significant challenge in biomechanics and robotics by enabling a single policy to control complex musculoskeletal models across multiple tasks and embodiments, potentially reducing the need for task-specific training. Arnold's key innovation is its sensorimotor vocabulary, a compositional representation of sensory modalities, objectives, and actuators, which is leveraged via a transformer architecture. The framework supports efficient multi-task, multi-embodiment learning and rapid adaptation to novel tasks, while causal probing revealed that low-dimensional muscle synergies remain largely task-specific.

## [Study Reveals Widespread Memorization in Medical LLMs, Raising Privacy and Safety Concerns](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.08604) ⭐️ 8.0/10

This preprint (arXiv:2509.08604) systematically analyzes memorization in medical LLMs across three adaptation scenarios, including continued pretraining, fine-tuning on benchmarks, and fine-tuning on over 13,000 real patient records from Yale New Haven Health System. It finds memorization is prevalent and significantly higher than in general domains, with up to 87% of pretraining memorized content persisting after fine-tuning. This work highlights critical risks for patient privacy and clinical safety, as LLMs may inadvertently reproduce sensitive patient details and produce overconfident, misleading outputs. The findings could influence how medical AI models are developed, regulated, and deployed, emphasizing the need for privacy-preserving techniques and careful evaluation of memorization. The study covers three adaptation scenarios: continued pretraining on medical corpora, fine-tuning on standard medical benchmarks, and fine-tuning on real-world clinical data. Memorization is persistent—up to 87% of content memorized during continued pretraining remains after fine-tuning on new tasks—and has distinct characteristics depending on the adaptation method.

The paper introduces Dynamic Jailbreaking Attack (DJA), a parameter-free gradient-based framework that dynamically adjusts optimization strategy and suffix length. DJA achieves a 100% attack success rate (ASR) across 40 safety-aligned LLMs from 12 model families, requiring only 13.68 optimization rounds on average. DJA addresses key limitations of static gradient-based jailbreak methods, improving both effectiveness and efficiency. This work could influence future LLM safety evaluations and adversarial robustness research, highlighting the need for more adaptive defenses. DJA uses dynamic candidate exploration, dynamic relevant targets, and a dynamic optimization strategy. It samples candidate target responses from the LLM's distribution, selects optimal targets via a multi-objective scorer, and adapts suffix length and iterations based on prompt difficulty.

## [RESample: Coverage-Guided Data Augmentation for VLA Failure Recovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.17640) ⭐️ 8.0/10

RESample is a novel coverage-guided data augmentation framework that generates failure recovery trajectories for Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models, addressing the lack of corrective supervision in standard demonstration datasets. It achieves up to 12% absolute improvement in success rates on LIBERO and real-world tasks with at most 20% additional samples. This work tackles a critical gap in VLA-based robotic manipulation: the inability to recover from execution deviations during deployment. By improving failure recovery, RESample enhances the robustness and real-world applicability of VLA policies, potentially accelerating their adoption in dynamic environments. RESample trains a conservative coverage function to identify failure cases within the data distribution but missing from successful demonstrations, then performs exploratory sampling to generate recovery actions. Experiments on LIBERO and real-world manipulation tasks show consistent gains, with up to 12% absolute improvement using no more than 20% additional samples.

**Background**: Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models combine visual perception, language understanding, and action generation, typically trained on large-scale imitation learning datasets of successful demonstrations. However, these datasets lack corrective supervision for failures, leading to distributional shift during deployment when the policy encounters deviations. Data augmentation techniques, such as coverage-guided methods, aim to expand training data coverage to improve robustness. RESample specifically targets failure recovery by generating exploratory trajectories that fill gaps in the data distribution.

## [Bi-Level RL Framework Minimizes Sim-to-Real Gap via Gradient-Based Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.17709) ⭐️ 8.0/10

This paper introduces a bi-level reinforcement learning (RL) approach that adapts simulation parameters using gradients of real-world policy performance, directly addressing the objective mismatch between predictive accuracy and task performance. The method is derived from sensitivity analysis of locally converged policies trained with Stochastic Policy Gradient (SPG) methods in an actor-critic setting, and is demonstrated through a proof-of-concept bi-level PPO algorithm. This work addresses a core challenge in sim-to-real RL by coupling simulation model adaptation with policy performance, potentially improving the deployment of RL policies in real-world applications where simulation is used to reduce costly interactions. It offers a novel perspective that could influence future research on objective mismatch and model adaptation in RL. The approach formulates a bi-level optimization problem where the inner loop trains a policy with SPG methods, and the outer loop updates simulation parameters using gradients of real-world policy performance. The paper provides a thorough convergence analysis of the proposed bi-level RL approach and illustrates the concept with a proof-of-concept bi-level PPO algorithm.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 6, 04:00

**Background**: Sim-to-real RL trains policies in simulation before deploying them in real environments, but discrepancies between simulation and reality, known as the sim-to-real gap, often degrade performance. Traditional simulation models are built for predictive accuracy, while policies aim to maximize task performance, creating an objective mismatch. Dyna-style model-based RL also relies on simulation models but faces similar model bias issues. Bi-level optimization in RL involves hierarchical learning where an outer loop optimizes parameters (e.g., simulation parameters) based on inner-loop policy performance.

<a id="item-104"></a>
## [Neural Diversity Reduces LLM Hallucinations by 25.6%](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.20690) ⭐️ 8.0/10

This paper introduces neural diversity, a mechanism using decorrelated parallel representations, and ND-LoRA, which combines parallel LoRA adapters with Barlow Twins regularization. It reduces hallucinations by up to 25.6% (14.6% on average) while preserving accuracy, and provides the first formal tail bounds for hallucination probability in ensembled language models. This work offers a new axis for improving LLM reliability beyond scaling parameters and data, which is crucial as hallucinations remain a major obstacle to deploying LLMs in high-stakes applications. The formal bounds and empirical gains could influence future model design and inspire further research into diversity-based regularization. The method explains 94.3% of empirical reliability variation across parallel configurations, and a 0.1% increase in neural correlation is associated with a 3.8% increase in hallucinations. Ablations show that LoRA adapters and regularization act synergistically, and causal interventions confirm neural diversity as the mediating factor; task-dependent optimal diversity levels emerge.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 6, 04:00

**Background**: Language models often generate plausible but incorrect content, known as hallucinations, despite scaling up parameters and data. LoRA (Low-Rank Adaptation) is a parameter-efficient fine-tuning method that freezes pretrained weights and injects trainable low-rank matrices. Barlow Twins is a self-supervised regularization technique that decorrelates representations. This paper combines these ideas to promote neural diversity, defined as decorrelated parallel representations, as a principled way to reduce hallucinations.

<a id="item-105"></a>
## [DeepThinkVLA: Unlocking Effective CoT Reasoning in VLA Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.15669) ⭐️ 8.0/10

The paper identifies two necessary conditions for effective Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning in Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models: decoding alignment and causal alignment. It introduces DeepThinkVLA, a model with a hybrid-attention decoder and a two-stage SFT-then-RL pipeline, achieving state-of-the-art results on LIBERO, LIBERO-Plus, and RoboTwin 2.0. This work provides a rigorous diagnosis of when and why CoT reasoning helps VLA models, addressing a critical question in embodied AI. The findings and the DeepThinkVLA architecture are likely to influence future research and development in robot learning, potentially leading to more robust and capable embodied agents. The paper shows that forcing CoT and actions through a single autoregressive decoder degrades performance by 4.2 percentage points, and that without causal alignment, supervised CoT leads to a 32.0 pp drop, nearly identical to the 31.6 pp drop of a reasoning-free baseline. DeepThinkVLA achieves 97.0% success on LIBERO, 79.0% robustness on LIBERO-Plus (vs. 61.6% for π0-FAST), and 59.3% success on RoboTwin 2.0, exceeding the strongest baseline by 21.7 points.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 6, 04:00

**Background**: Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models are a class of multimodal foundation models that integrate vision, language, and action, typically built by fine-tuning a vision-language model on robot trajectories. Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning involves generating intermediate reasoning steps before predicting actions, aiming to improve performance and interpretability. However, prior CoT-VLA systems reported inconsistent gains, and this paper systematically investigates the conditions under which CoT is beneficial.

## [Chain-of-Visual-Thought Boosts VLMs with Continuous Visual Tokens](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.19418) ⭐️ 8.0/10

The paper introduces Chain-of-Visual-Thought (COVT), a framework that enables vision-language models (VLMs) to reason through continuous visual tokens, not just words. Integrating COVT into models like Qwen2.5-VL and LLaVA improves performance by 3% to 16% across over ten perception benchmarks. This addresses a key limitation of VLMs in dense visual perception tasks like spatial reasoning and geometry, which are crucial for real-world applications. By enabling more precise and interpretable multimodal reasoning, COVT could advance the field of multimodal AI and improve downstream tasks such as robotics and autonomous driving. COVT uses approximately 20 continuous visual tokens per image, distilling knowledge from lightweight vision experts to capture 2D appearance, 3D geometry, spatial layout, and edge structure. During training, the VLM autoregressively predicts these tokens to reconstruct dense supervision signals like depth, segmentation, edges, and DINO features, while at inference it reasons directly in the continuous token space.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 6, 04:00

**Background**: Vision-language models (VLMs) typically reason in linguistic space, which limits their ability to handle dense visual information. Continuous visual tokens are compact latent representations that encode rich perceptual cues, allowing models to reason more effectively about spatial and geometric details. DINO features are self-supervised visual features that capture high-level semantic information, often used for tasks like segmentation and depth estimation.

## [LLM Generalization Across Difficulty Levels Is Limited](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.21692) ⭐️ 8.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2511.21692) systematically evaluates LLM generalization across task difficulties using Item Response Theory (IRT) and outputs from thousands of models, finding that cross-difficulty generalization is often limited. This finding challenges the assumption that training on easy or hard data alone can improve performance across all difficulty levels, impacting data curation and evaluation practices in LLM development. The study ranks examples in six datasets using IRT-based difficulty ratings derived solely from model outputs, excluding human opinions. It reveals that neither easy-to-hard nor hard-to-easy generalization is consistent, emphasizing the need for diverse difficulty in training and evaluation data.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 6, 04:00

**Background**: Item Response Theory (IRT) is a statistical framework used in educational testing to model the relationship between a person's latent ability and their responses to test items of varying difficulty. In this context, IRT is applied to LLMs to objectively measure task difficulty based on the performance of many models, avoiding human-centric bias.

## [Feedback Loops and LLM Choice in C-to-Rust Translation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.02567) ⭐️ 8.0/10

This paper presents a case study on an automated C-to-Rust translation system, examining how feedback loops, LLM selection, and behavior-preserving code changes affect translation quality. The study finds that feedback loops diminish performance differences across LLMs and that code perturbations can improve system performance. This research provides empirical insights into optimizing LLM-based code translation systems, which are increasingly important for industrial adoption. The findings could guide tooling and research in automated code migration, particularly for safety-critical languages like Rust. The translation system uses a generate-and-check pattern, where LLM-generated Rust code is automatically checked for compilability and behavioral equivalence with the original C code. Negative results trigger a feedback loop for repair. The study varies LLM choice, feedback loops, and code perturbations to measure their impact on translation success rates.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 6, 04:00

**Background**: C-to-Rust translation is attractive due to Rust's safety guarantees, but automated translation requires high reliability. Existing tools like C2Rust produce unsafe Rust code that mirrors C, while LLM-based approaches can generate more idiomatic code but need verification. The generate-and-check pattern is a common approach to ensure correctness, and behavior-preserving code changes are transformations that keep external behavior unchanged.

## [HERO: Hierarchical Evidential Reasoning Optimization for Radiology Report Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.03321) ⭐️ 8.0/10

The paper introduces HERO, a factorized policy optimization framework for radiology report generation that aligns heterogeneous supervision via segment-, token-, and completion-level optimization. It outperforms strong baselines on MIMIC-CXR and IU-Xray, achieving state-of-the-art clinical efficacy and reducing clinical hallucinations. This work addresses a critical challenge in medical AI by improving the reliability of radiology report generation, which is essential for clinical decision-making. The hierarchical optimization approach could inspire similar techniques in other domains where heterogeneous supervision and hallucination mitigation are important. HERO uses a heterogeneous reward formulation covering diagnostic accuracy, reasoning quality, and think-answer consistency, and optimizes reasoning, diagnosis, and evidence grounding separately. Experiments on MIMIC-CXR and IU-Xray show improvements over supervised and RL baselines, with more evidence-grounded and think-answer-consistent reports.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 6, 04:00

**Background**: Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have advanced Radiology Report Generation (RRG), but aligning them via reinforcement learning is challenging due to heterogeneous medical supervision. Vanilla Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) assigns uniform credit across the entire generation, leading to issues like segment interference and token dilution. HERO addresses these by factorizing the optimization into multiple granularities. The MIMIC-CXR and IU-Xray datasets are standard benchmarks for chest X-ray report generation.

<a id="item-110"></a>
## [MT-GRPO: Balancing Multi-Task LLM Reasoning with Dynamic Weights](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.05547) ⭐️ 8.0/10

The paper introduces Multi-Task GRPO (MT-GRPO), an algorithm that dynamically adapts task weights to optimize worst-task performance and uses a ratio-preserving sampler to align gradient contributions with these weights. Experiments show MT-GRPO achieves 16-28% and 6% absolute improvement in worst-task accuracy over standard GRPO and DAPO, respectively, while requiring 50% fewer training steps in a 3-task setting. This work addresses a critical challenge in RL-based post-training for LLMs: balancing performance across diverse reasoning tasks, which is essential for real-world deployment. By improving worst-task performance without sacrificing average accuracy, MT-GRPO could enable more reliable and efficient multi-task training, influencing future RL algorithms and applications. MT-GRPO uses dynamic task weighting to explicitly optimize worst-task performance and a ratio-preserving sampler to ensure task-wise policy gradients reflect the adapted weights. The method was validated on 3-task and 9-task settings, showing consistent improvements in worst-task accuracy while maintaining competitive average accuracy.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 6, 04:00

**Background**: GRPO (Group Relative Policy Optimization) is a reinforcement learning algorithm used in LLM post-training, which compares groups of responses to compute advantages without a critic model. However, a straightforward multi-task adaptation often leads to imbalanced performance, as some tasks dominate optimization while others stagnate, and tasks vary in how often prompts yield zero advantages. MT-GRPO addresses these issues by dynamically adjusting task weights and using a sampler that preserves the intended weight ratios in gradient updates.

## [Survey on Agent Memory for Self-Evolving, Long-Horizon Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.06052) ⭐️ 8.0/10

This survey provides a unified view of foundation agent memory along three dimensions: memory substrate, cognitive mechanism, and memory subject, and analyzes memory operations under single- and multi-agent topologies. It highlights a paradigm shift from model-centric to problem-centric research, emphasizing memory as the substrate for self-evolving agents. As AI research shifts towards real-world utility in long-horizon, dynamic settings, memory emerges as a critical solution to context explosion. This survey synthesizes hundreds of 2025 papers, offering a roadmap for developing agents that can continuously learn and adapt, which is essential for applications like agentic coding and deep research. The survey categorizes memory into internal parametric state and external retrieval-augmented stores, and cognitive mechanisms including sensory, working, episodic, semantic, and procedural memory. It also discusses learning policies over memory operations, such as reinforcement-learned context curation and experience consolidation, and reviews evaluation benchmarks and metrics for memory utility.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 6, 04:00

**Background**: LLM-based agents face context explosion when handling long-horizon tasks that require many sequential steps, as fixed context windows cannot hold all information. Memory systems, including external vector databases and episodic memory, enable agents to store and selectively reuse information across interactions. This survey addresses the growing need for structured understanding of memory design in the 'second half' of AI research, where real-world evaluation and problem definition take precedence.

## [Post-Training Boosts Small LLMs to Outperform Larger Causal Reasoners](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.06337) ⭐️ 8.0/10

This paper introduces CauGym, a benchmark for causal reasoning, and demonstrates that post-training with methods like SFT, DPO, KTO, PPO, and GRPO enables smaller LLMs to achieve competitive or superior causal inference performance compared to larger models. Notably, a 14B parameter model reached 93.5% accuracy on the CaLM benchmark, surpassing OpenAI o3's 55.4%. This finding challenges the assumption that larger models are always better for complex reasoning tasks, suggesting that targeted post-training can be a cost-effective alternative. It has practical implications for deploying efficient LLMs in causal inference applications, potentially reducing computational costs while maintaining high performance. The study systematically evaluates five post-training approaches across seven core causal tasks and five test sets, showing strong generalization and robustness under distribution shifts and noisy data. The authors provide their data and GRPO-model publicly at https://github.com/OpenCausaLab/CauGym.

Post-training methods like supervised fine-tuning (SFT), direct preference optimization (DPO), and reinforcement learning (PPO, GRPO) are used to adapt pre-trained models for specific tasks.

This paper presents a formal framework for analyzing and securing agentic AI skills, including proofs and an open-source tool called SkillFortify. The revised version corrects experimental results and external claims, with all experiments re-measured against the released v0.6.0 implementation. As agentic AI systems increasingly rely on third-party skills, supply chain security becomes critical. This work provides formal guarantees and a practical tool, addressing a timely gap in AI security and potentially influencing how skills are vetted and deployed. The paper includes 5 theorems with full proofs and 68 references. The v2 corrections clarify that CVE-2026-25253 is an authentication-token exfiltration issue, not RCE, and that E3 reverses to a negative result, showing information flow analysis adds no detections over pattern matching on this corpus.

This paper discovers that video diffusion models encode physical plausibility signals in intermediate layers and introduces lightweight verifiers for inference-time trajectory selection and guidance. This work provides a new way to improve physical consistency in generated videos without fine-tuning, which could benefit generative AI and video understanding applications. It also offers practical methods that reduce inference time while maintaining quality. The authors probe intermediate denoising representations of pretrained Diffusion Transformers (DiTs) and find that physically plausible and implausible videos are partially separable in mid-layer feature space. They propose progressive trajectory selection and reward-gradient guidance, achieving a 37% reduction in wall-clock inference time on CogVideoX-2B while matching verifier-based Best-of-4 performance.

This paper builds on these concepts to probe and exploit physical plausibility signals in DiTs.

## [Luna: A C++ Bound Propagator Outperforming alpha-CROWN for Neural Network Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.23878) ⭐️ 8.0/10

Luna, a new C++ abstract-interpretation-based bound propagator, has been introduced, supporting Interval Bound Propagation, DeepPoly/CROWN, and alpha-CROWN analyses over a general computational graph. It outperforms the state-of-the-art alpha-CROWN implementation in both bound tightness and computational efficiency on VNN-COMP 2025 benchmarks. This is significant because existing alpha-CROWN implementations are Python-based, complicating integration into production systems. Luna's C++ implementation offers better performance and easier integration, potentially advancing the practical adoption of neural network verification in real-world applications. Luna supports a wide range of layer types including fully connected, convolutional, transposed convolutional, batch normalization, ReLU, sigmoid, reshape, flatten, slice, concatenation, and residual connections. It is publicly available on GitHub at https://github.com/ai-ar-research/luna.
